## 🤖🔧 Assistant
<details>
<summary>Write Users/sethml/.claude/projects/-Users-sethml-src-opencascade-rs/memory/libclang-template-instantiation.md</summary>

```markdown
---
name: libclang-template-instantiation
description: How to bind OCCT template-instantiation aliases, and the libclang limitation that makes it non-obvious
metadata:
  type: project
---

Binding an OCCT alias for a template instantiation (`using GeomLProp_SLProps =
GeomLProp_SLPropsBase<occ::handle<Geom_Surface>>;`) requires working around **two**
libclang behaviours. Implemented 2026-07-30 in `parser.rs::parse_template_classes`,
opt-in via `generate_methods` in `[template_instantiations]`.

1. A specialization's members only materialize if something forces instantiation.
   `parse_headers` appends `template class <spelling>;` to the synthetic TU.
   **`static_assert(sizeof(Alias) > 0)` is NOT enough** — verified: the specialization
   stays `completeDefinition: false` with 0 members.

2. **Even then, libclang never exposes the substituted members.** The specialization
   cursor's children are only NamespaceRef/TemplateRef/TypeRef (the template arguments).
   The members live on the *primary template* cursor, unsubstituted. So the fix is: parse
   the primary template and substitute parameters yourself, via `template_params` config →
   thread-local consulted in `class_type()` (the one place a named type becomes
   `Type::Class`).

**Trap that cost real time:** `clang++ -Xclang -ast-dump=json` DOES show the fully
substituted members (21 methods with `handle<Geom_Surface>` resolved). That is Clang's own
AST, which is strictly richer than libclang's cursor API. **Do not use an ast-dump probe to

spellings — clang canonicalizes those (expands default args, `occ::`→`opencascade::`), so
config spellings rarely match verbatim.
```

## 🤖 Assistant
<!-- msg_011CdaFP1gE6DKxuk98ztPCj -->

Done — committed as `b211e47`.

## First, a correction to my estimate

My feasibility probe was flawed and it cost part of the plan. I validated with `clang++ -Xclang -ast-dump=json`, which showed 21 fully-substituted methods on the specialization — but that's **Clang's own AST**, which is strictly richer than libclang's cursor API that the generator actually uses. Through libclang, the specialization cursor's children are only `NamespaceRef`/`TemplateRef`/`TypeRef` — the template arguments. Zero members. I proved the data exists, not that the generator could reach it.

I also confirmed `static_assert(sizeof(Alias) > 0)` is insufficient — the specialization stays `completeDefinition: false`. Only an explicit `template class X<Args>;` materializes anything.

## What actually works

Members live on the **primary template**, unsubstituted. So the generator parses that and substitutes parameters itself:

- `parse_headers` appends `template class <spelling>;` to the synthetic TU.
- Instantiations are matched via their **alias declaration**, not spelling comparison — clang canonicalizes spellings (expands default args, `occ::`→`opencascade::`), so config rarely matches verbatim.
- `template_params` maps each parameter to a concrete type, installed as a thread-local consulted by `class_type()` — the single point where a named type becomes `Type::Class`.

That last piece is the part I hadn't budgeted for, and it's why the config requires an explicit `template_params` mapping rather than inferring it.

## Result

`GeomLProp_SLProps` and `BRepLProp_SLProps`: **21 methods + 3 constructors each, zero skips**. The full differential-geometry API — `normal()`, `gaussian_curvature()`, `mean_curvature()`, `max/min_curvature()`, `is_umbilic()`, `curvature_directions()`, tangents, `d1u`…`duv`.

Critically, `set_surface` and the surface-taking constructors bind to the substituted type (`Handle_Geom_Surface` / `b_rep_adaptor::Surface`), so the objects are actually constructible. Without substitution they'd have been skipped and the class would have been a 21-method dead end.

Side benefit: `GeomPlate`, `LocalAnalysis`, and `BRepFill` methods that returned `ffi_types::GeomLProp_SLProps` now return `geom_l_prop::SLProps`, so callers can use the result.

## Verification

[tests/surface_local_props.rs](crates/opencascade-sys/tests/surface_local_props.rs) checks numbers, not linkage — a sphere of radius R must have Gaussian curvature 1/R² and principal curvatures of magnitude 1/R, its normal must be radial, and a planar face must be flat and umbilic. All pass to 1e-9. Full protocol green: 22 generator tests, whole workspace, 0 deprecation warnings.

## The prediction that held

Skips stayed at **721**. This recovered no previously-skipped symbols, exactly as I said it wouldn't — those members were never parsed, so never recorded as skips. The gain is entirely classes becoming usable.

That means the 426 `NCollection<occ::handle<T>>` skips are still the biggest coverage win available, and they need the *other* fix — extending collection discovery to handle elements. The machinery I just built doesn't help there. Say the word if you want that next.

```
/// Helper to extract module name from a type (e.g., "topo_ds" from "TopoDS_Shape")
```

**After:**
```
/// If `spelling` names an OCCT handle (`occ::handle<X>` or
/// `opencascade::handle<X>`), return the pointee class `X`.
pub fn handle_inner(spelling: &str) -> Option<String> {
    let s = spelling.trim();
    let rest = s
        .strip_prefix("occ::handle<")
        .or_else(|| s.strip_prefix("opencascade::handle<"))
        .or_else(|| s.strip_prefix("Handle("))?;
    let inner = if s.starts_with("Handle(") {
        rest.strip_suffix(')')?
    } else {
        rest.strip_suffix('>')?
    };
    let inner = inner.trim();
    // Nested handles (a handle to a collection, say) are not element types we can
    // name cleanly; leave those to the normal unbindable path.
    if inner.is_empty() || inner.contains('<') {
        return None;
    }
    Some(inner.to_string())
}

/// Canonical token for a collection element/value type: handles collapse to the
/// `Handle_X` spelling, which is a real type on both sides — a C++ typedef and the
/// generated Rust `ffi_types::Handle_X` — so the rest of the generation needs no
/// special cases. Everything else passes through unchanged.
pub fn canonical_element_token(spelling: &str) -> String {
    match handle_inner(spelling) {
        Some(inner) => crate::type_mapping::handle_type_name(&inner),
        None => spelling.to_string(),
    }
}

/// Helper to extract module name from a type (e.g., "topo_ds" from "TopoDS_Shape")
```

## 🤖💭 Assistant
<!-- msg_011CdaL4HMram5ubiDuXxiPc -->

> The issue is that handle-managed collections reference types that aren't being generated in the FFI types because the collection's typedef name changed when it was renamed to include the Handle_ prefix, causing a mismatch between how the handle-type registration is keyed (using the original spelling from template instantiations) and what's actually being generated.
> 
> I'm considering two approaches: either apply the same canonicalization consistently across both the handle-type registration and the collection naming so they agree, or avoid renaming and stick with the mangled raw spelling. The first option produces cleaner names but requires ensuring the canonicalization is applied uniformly in the template alias mapping, ideally by baking the element canonicalization directly into the template_alias_name function so any handle element inside a template gets processed the same way.
> 
> Looking at the template_alias_name function, I can normalize `occ::handle<X>` to `Handle_X` before the mangling step, which would make the collection name and handle registration match automatically. This is a single central change that solves the consistency problem, and I can remove the separate naming_spelling rebuild I added earlier since the template_alias_name function would handle it.
> 
> I'm implementing a helper function that uses regex to collapse handle spellings — converting patterns like `occ::handle<Geom_Curve>` to `Handle_Geom_Curve` — and calling it at the start of template_alias_name so both the collection generator and handle registration produce the same names.


## 🤖 Assistant
<!-- msg_011CdaL4HMram5ubiDuXxiPc -->

The rename broke the link with the registered handle-type name. The fix belongs in one place — `template_alias_name`, which both sides go through:


## 🤖🔧 Assistant
<details>
<summary>Edit crates/opencascade-binding-generator/src/config.rs</summary>

**Before:**
```
pub fn template_alias_name(template_spelling: &str) -> String {
    template_spelling
        .replace("::", "_")
```

**After:**
```
/// Collapse OCCT handle spellings inside a template argument list, so
/// `NCollection_HArray1<occ::handle<Geom_Curve>>` mangles to
/// `NCollection_HArray1_Handle_Geom_Curve` rather than the unreadable
/// `NCollection_HArray1_occ_handle_Geom_Curve`.
///
/// Both collection discovery and handle-type registration name themselves through
/// `template_alias_name`, so doing this here keeps the two in agreement — a
/// collection named `..._Handle_Geom_Curve` needs its handle type to be called
/// `Handle_NCollection_HArray1_Handle_Geom_Curve`.
fn collapse_handle_args(spelling: &str) -> String {
    use std::sync::OnceLock;
    static RE: OnceLock<regex::Regex> = OnceLock::new();
    let re = RE.get_or_init(|| {
        regex::Regex::new(r"(?:occ|opencascade)::handle<\s*([A-Za-z_][A-Za-z0-9_:]*)\s*>").unwrap()
    });
    re.replace_all(spelling, |caps: &regex::Captures| {
        format!("Handle_{}", caps[1].replace("::", "_"))
    })
    .into_owned()
}

pub fn template_alias_name(template_spelling: &str) -> String {
    collapse_handle_args(template_spelling)
        .replace("::", "_")
```
</details>

Found it: the filter whitelists `opencascade::handle<` but the parser reports `occ::handle<`, which then hits the "nested type with template" rejection. One-line fix:


## 🤖🔧 Assistant
<details>
<summary>Edit crates/opencascade-binding-generator/src/main.rs</summary>

**Before:**
```
            // Handle wrapper: opencascade::handle<Foo>
            if arg.starts_with("opencascade::handle<") {
                continue;
            }
```

**After:**
```
            // Handle wrapper: opencascade::handle<Foo>. Clang reports the `occ::`
            // spelling in most signatures, and without it these fall through to the
            // "nested type with template" rejection below — which is what kept
            // handle-element collections (NCollection_List<occ::handle<T>>, …) out
            // of collection discovery entirely.
            if arg.starts_with("opencascade::handle<") || arg.starts_with("occ::handle<") {
                continue;
            }
```

```
//! Tests for collections whose element type is an OCCT handle.
//!
//! Collection discovery previously rejected `NCollection_List<occ::handle<T>>` and
//! friends, so any method mentioning one was skipped. These tests exercise a
//! handle-element collection end to end — store handles, read them back, and
//! confirm the round-tripped handle still refers to the same live object — rather
//! than only checking that the bindings compile and link.

use opencascade_sys::gp;
use opencascade_sys::n_collection::{List_Handle_Geom_Curve, Sequence_Handle_Geom_Curve};

/// A `Handle(Geom_Curve)` for the line through `origin` along +X.
fn line_handle(origin: (f64, f64, f64)) -> opencascade_sys::OwnedPtr<opencascade_sys::ffi_types::Handle_Geom_Curve> {
    let p = gp::Pnt::new_real3(origin.0, origin.1, origin.2);
    let d = gp::Dir::new_real3(1.0, 0.0, 0.0);
    let line = opencascade_sys::geom::Line::new_pnt_dir(&p, &d);
    let h = opencascade_sys::geom::Line::to_handle(line);
    h.to_handle_curve()
}

#[test]
fn sequence_of_handles_round_trips() {
    let mut seq = Sequence_Handle_Geom_Curve::new();
    assert_eq!(seq.size(), 0);

    seq.append(&line_handle((0.0, 0.0, 0.0)));
    seq.append(&line_handle((0.0, 7.0, 0.0)));
    assert_eq!(seq.size(), 2, "both handles should be stored");

    // Reading back a handle must yield a live curve, not a dangling or null one:
    // evaluate it and check the point we expect on that line.
    let second = seq.value(2);
    assert!(!second.is_null(), "stored handle should not be null");
    let curve = second.get();
    let p = curve.value(0.0);
    assert!(
        (p.y() - 7.0).abs() < 1e-12,
        "round-tripped handle should be the second line (y=7), got y={}",
        p.y()
    );
}

#[test]
fn list_of_handles_holds_ownership() {
    let mut list = List_Handle_Geom_Curve::new();
    // Drop each handle right after appending: the collection must own its own
    // reference, so the underlying curves have to outlive the locals.
    for y in [1.0, 2.0, 3.0] {
        let h = line_handle((0.0, y, 0.0));
        list.append(&h);
    }
    assert_eq!(list.size(), 3);

    // Iterate and confirm every element is still a live curve.
    let mut seen = Vec::new();
    for h in list.iter() {
        assert!(!h.is_null(), "iterated handle should not be null");
        seen.push(h.get().value(0.0).y());
    }
    seen.sort_by(|a, b| a.partial_cmp(b).unwrap());
    assert_eq!(seen.len(), 3);
    for (got, want) in seen.iter().zip([1.0, 2.0, 3.0]) {
        assert!(
            (got - want).abs() < 1e-12,
            "expected line at y={want}, got {got}"
        );
    }

    list.clear();
    assert_eq!(list.size(), 0);
}

```
</details>

## 🤖🔧 Assistant
<details>
<summary>Write crates/opencascade-sys/tests/handle_collections.rs</summary>

```
//! Tests for collections whose element type is an OCCT handle.
//!
//! Collection discovery previously rejected `NCollection_List<occ::handle<T>>` and
//! friends, so every method mentioning one was skipped. These tests exercise a
//! handle-element collection end to end — store handles, read them back, and
//! confirm the round-tripped handle still refers to a live object — rather than
//! only checking that the bindings compile and link.

use opencascade_sys::geom::{Handle_Geom_Curve, Line};
use opencascade_sys::gp;
use opencascade_sys::n_collection::Sequence_Handle_Geom_Curve;
use opencascade_sys::OwnedPtr;

/// A `Handle(Geom_Curve)` for the line through (0, y, 0) along +X.
fn line_at_y(y: f64) -> OwnedPtr<Handle_Geom_Curve> {
    let p = gp::Pnt::new_real3(0.0, y, 0.0);
    let d = gp::Dir::new_real3(1.0, 0.0, 0.0);
    Line::to_handle(Line::new_pnt_dir(&p, &d)).to_handle_curve()
}

#[test]
fn sequence_of_handles_round_trips() {
    let mut seq = Sequence_Handle_Geom_Curve::new();
    assert_eq!(seq.size(), 0);

    seq.append(&line_at_y(0.0));
    seq.append(&line_at_y(7.0));
    assert_eq!(seq.size(), 2, "both handles should be stored");

    // A read-back handle must yield a live curve, not a null or dangling one:
    // evaluate it and check the point we expect on that line.
    let second = seq.value(2);
    assert!(!second.is_null(), "stored handle should not be null");
    let p = second.get().value(0.0);
    assert!(
        (p.y() - 7.0).abs() < 1e-12,
        "round-tripped handle should be the second line (y=7), got y={}",
        p.y()
    );
}

#[test]
fn sequence_of_handles_owns_its_elements() {
    let mut seq = Sequence_Handle_Geom_Curve::new();
    // Each handle is dropped right after being appended, so the collection must
    // hold its own reference for the curves to survive.
    for y in [1.0, 2.0, 3.0] {
        let h = line_at_y(y);
        seq.append(&h);
    }
    assert_eq!(seq.size(), 3);

    let mut ys: Vec<f64> = seq
        .iter()
        .map(|h| {
            assert!(!h.is_null(), "iterated handle should not be null");
            h.get().value(0.0).y()
        })
        .collect();
    ys.sort_by(|a, b| a.partial_cmp(b).unwrap());

    assert_eq!(ys.len(), 3, "iterator should yield every element");
    for (got, want) in ys.iter().zip([1.0, 2.0, 3.0]) {
        assert!(
            (got - want).abs() < 1e-12,
            "expected a line at y={want}, got {got}"
        );
    }

    seq.clear();
    assert_eq!(seq.size(), 0);
}

```
</details>

```
canonical types rather than the deprecated aliases.

## Skipped Symbols

The binding generator currently skips **~720** symbols (methods, constructors, static methods, and free functions) that it cannot safely represent in Rust FFI — out of ~144,000 bound functions. Abstract class constructors are silently omitted since that's expected behavior, not a binding limitation. Every other skipped symbol is documented in generated per-module \`.rs\` files as a \`// SKIPPED:\` comment block including:

- **Source location** (header file, line number, C++ symbol name)
- **Documentation comment** from the C++ header (first 3 lines)
- **Skip reason** explaining why the symbol was excluded
- **Commented-out Rust stub** showing the best-guess declaration

Example from \`select_mgr.rs\`:
\`\`\`rust
// SKIPPED: **Source:** \`SelectMgr_BVHThreadPool.hxx\`:101 - \`SelectMgr_BVHThreadPool::Threads\`
//   method: Returns array of threads
//   Reason: has unbindable types: return: unresolved template type (NCollection_Array1<BVHThread>&)
//   // pub fn threads(&mut self) -> /* NCollection_Array1<BVHThread>& */;
\`\`\`

### Skip Reason Breakdown

| Approx. count | Category | Description |
|------:|----------|-------------|
| ~460 | **Unbindable types** | A parameter/return type isn't in the resolved known-type set — internal helper types, unresolved templates, or platform/external-dependency types (X11 \`GLXFBConfig\`/\`Aspect_XDisplay*\`, macOS \`NSOpenGLContext*\`, FFmpeg/glTF writer types, etc.). |
| ~120 | **\`BRepGraphInc\` graph API** | New-in-8.0 experimental graph package with private nested types. |
| ~33 | **\`std::\` types** | e.g. \`std::string_view\` parameters. |
| ~14 | **Rvalue reference** | C++ move semantics (\`T&&\`) — const-ref overloads usually exist. |

Handle-wrapped \`NCollection_H*<T>\` instantiations (\`Handle(NCollection_HArray1<T>)\`, etc.)
used to dominate this list; they are now auto-registered as handle-able (see the
[handle collections](#handle-support) note below), leaving only a handful of skips.

Fixed-size arrays (\`T (&)[N]\`, \`const T (&)[N]\`, and \`T arr[N]\` parameter syntax) are bindable; there are **no** remaining skips categorized as C-style arrays. Core geometry (\`gp\`/\`Geom\`/\`Geom2d\`) and topology/modeling modules are essentially fully bound.

### How Skipped Symbols Are Tracked
```

## 🤖 Assistant
<!-- msg_011CdaLqzJU9Kp5TyL3xSfqW -->

All green. Committing:


## 🤖🔧 Assistant
<details>
<summary>Write tmp/commit-handle-collections.txt</summary>

```
Discover collections whose element type is a handle (721 -> 336 skips)

Collections with a handle element — `NCollection_List<occ::handle<T>>`,
`Sequence<occ::handle<T>>`, `DataMap<K, occ::handle<V>>` and so on — were never
generated, so every method mentioning one was skipped. At 426 symbols this was by
far the largest remaining skip category, roughly 59% of all skips.

Two independent causes, both fixed here.

The first is a one-line whitelist gap in `collect_template_class_types`. It
accepted a template argument spelled `opencascade::handle<...>`, but clang reports
the `occ::` spelling in most signatures, and `occ::handle<T>` then fell through to
the "nested type with template" rejection just below. So these spellings never
even reached collection discovery.

The second is that discovery had no notion of a handle element. `header_of` was
asked to resolve `occ::handle<Geom_Curve>`, which is not a class with a header of
its own, and the collection was dropped.

Handle arguments are now collapsed to the `Handle_X` token, which is a real type
on both sides — a C++ typedef and the generated Rust `ffi_types::Handle_X` — so
the rest of the generation needs no special cases: `append(&Handle_Geom_Curve)`,
`value(i) -> &Handle_Geom_Curve`, `iter()` yielding owned handles. Specifically:

  - The collapse lives in `config::template_alias_name`, which both collection
    discovery and handle-type registration go through. Doing it anywhere else
    desynchronises the two, and a collection named `..._Handle_Geom_Curve` whose
    handle type is still called `..._occ_handle_Geom_Curve` does not compile.
  - Element/value headers resolve through the pointee class, and the element's
    module comes from it too (`Handle_Geom_Curve` belongs to `geom`).
  - The collection's C++ block re-emits `typedef opencascade::handle<X> Handle_X;`.
    Per-toolkit handle typedefs only cover handles the group's own classes use,
    which need not include this collection's element. Repeating an identical
    typedef is legal C++, so this is safe when both are present.

Collections go 221 -> 380 (+159: 64 HArray1, 25 Sequence, 20 HSequence, 16 List,
10 Array1, 9 DataMap, 3 Map, 3 IndexedMap, and a few more), and skipped symbols
721 -> 336. Nothing was renamed away: the churn in n_collection.rs is ordering.

Of the ~55 template skips left, most are OCCT `::Iterator` parameters — which the
generated `iter()` supersedes — and `std::optional`/`std::array`, a separate
value-mapping problem.

tests/handle_collections.rs exercises the path rather than just linking it: it
stores handles in a sequence, reads one back and evaluates the curve to confirm
it is the expected line, and appends handles that are dropped immediately
afterwards to confirm the collection holds its own reference.

Co-Authored-By: Claude Opus 4.8 <noreply@anthropic.com>

```
</details>

## 🤖 Assistant
<!-- msg_011CdaLte3PfTBcnXiKBEsUo -->

Done — committed as `e0a1c7f`.

## Result: skipped symbols 721 → 336

The 426-symbol category I flagged as the best remaining ROI is now largely gone — **385 symbols recovered**, and collections went **221 → 380 (+159)**.

## Two causes, one of them embarrassing

**A one-line whitelist gap.** `collect_template_class_types` accepted template arguments spelled `opencascade::handle<...>` — but clang reports the `occ::` spelling in most signatures, so `occ::handle<T>` fell straight through to the "nested type with template" rejection immediately below it. These spellings never reached collection discovery at all. That single missing alternative accounted for most of the 426.

**No handle-element concept in discovery.** `header_of` was asked to resolve `occ::handle<Geom_Curve>` — not a class with a header of its own — so the collection was dropped.

## The design point that matters

Handle arguments collapse to the `Handle_X` token, which is a real type on both sides (a C++ typedef and the generated `ffi_types::Handle_X`), so the rest of codegen needed no special cases. The collapse had to go in `config::template_alias_name` specifically — both collection discovery *and* handle-type registration derive names through it. I first put it only in the collection path, and got exactly the predictable failure: a collection named `..._Handle_Geom_Curve` whose handle type was still `..._occ_handle_Geom_Curve`. Build errors across the board.

The collection's C++ block also re-emits `typedef opencascade::handle<X> Handle_X;`, since per-toolkit handle typedefs only cover handles that group's own classes use — which needn't include another group's collection element. Repeating an identical typedef is legal C++, so it's safe when both are present.

## Verification

[tests/handle_collections.rs](crates/opencascade-sys/tests/handle_collections.rs) exercises the path rather than just linking it: stores handles in a sequence, reads one back and evaluates the curve to confirm it's the expected line, and appends handles that are dropped immediately after to confirm the collection holds its own reference. Both pass.

Full protocol green — clean build (0 Rust errors, 0 C++ errors, 0 deprecation warnings), 22 generator tests, whole workspace, 0 failures. I also verified the 14k-line churn in `n_collection.rs` was reordering, not removals: every previously-generated collection still exists.

## What's left

Of ~55 remaining template skips, most are OCCT `::Iterator` parameters — which the generated `iter()` already supersedes — plus `std::optional`/`std::array`, a separate value-mapping problem. The largest remaining blocks are now `BRepGraph`/`BRepGraphInc` (~149, the experimental 8.0 graph package with private nested types), `std::` types (~49), `Extrema_PC` (~41), and `TCollection` (~33). README statistics updated to match.

One untracked directory appeared during the session, `claude-conversations/` — not mine and not committed; you may want to gitignore it.
